Symmetric graphs of prime valency with a transitive simple group Thanks: 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification. 05C25, 20B25, 20D40 Thanks: Supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (11861012, 11971248, 11731002) and the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities.
Jing Jian Li, Zai Ping Lu

Abstract
. A graph Γ = ( V , E ) \Gamma=(V,E) is called a Cayley graph of some group T T if the automorphism group 𝐀𝐮𝐭 ⁡ ( Γ ) \mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma) contains a subgroup T T which acts on regularly on V V . If the subgroup T T is normal in 𝐀𝐮𝐭 ⁡ ( Γ ) \mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma) then Γ \Gamma is called a normal Cayley graph of T T . Let r r be an odd prime. Fang et al. [ 12 ] proved that, with a finite number of exceptions for finite simple group T T , every connected symmetric Cayley graph of T T of valency r r is normal. In this paper, employing maximal factorizations of finite almost simple groups, we work out a possible list of those exceptions for T T . Keywords . Symmetric graph, Cayley graph, simple group, group factorization.



1.
Introduction

All graphs in this paper are assumed to be finite, simple
and undirected.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a graph with vertex set
V
and edge set
E
. Denote by
\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
the automorphism group of
\Gamma
, and let
G\leq\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
.
The graph
\Gamma
is called

G
-vertex transitive if

G
acts transitively on
V
, and called a Cayley graph of
G
if
G
acts regularly on
V
.
For
\alpha\in V
, let
G_{\alpha}=\{g\in G\mid\alpha^{g}=\alpha\}
and
\Gamma(\alpha)=\{\beta\in V\mid\{\alpha,\beta\}\in E\}
,
called the stabilizer and neighborhood
of
\alpha
in
G
and in
\Gamma
, respectively.
If
\Gamma
is
G
-vertex transitive and
G_{\alpha}
acts transitively on

\Gamma(\alpha)
then
\Gamma
is called
G
-symmetric.

In the literature, the solution of quite a number of problems on symmetric graphs have been reduced or partially reduced into
the class of graphs arising from almost simple groups. For example, the reduction for symmetric graphs of prime valency
[
34
]
, the reduction for
2
-arc-transitive graphs established
in
[
38
]
, the Weiss Conjecture
[
47
, Conjecture 3.12]
for non-bipartite locally primitive graphs
[
6
]
, the normality of Cayley graphs of simple groups

[
12
,
13
]
, the existence and classification of edge-primitive graphs
[
18
,
36
]
, and so on. Certainly, the class of graphs arising from almost simple groups plays an important role
in the theory of symmetric graphs.
In this paper, we will study those symmetric graphs admitting a transitive simple group.

Our main motivation comes from the study of the automorphism groups or the normality of symmetric Cayley graphs of simple groups. (Recall that a Cayley graph
\Gamma
of a group
T
is normal
[
48
]
if
T
is a normal subgroup of
\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
.)
Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a Cayley graph of some simple group
T
of prime valency
r\geq 3
. Assume that
\Gamma
is connected and
G
-symmetric, where
T\leq G\leq\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
. Then the Weiss Conjecture is true for
\Gamma
, that is, the orders of vertex-stabilizers have an upper bound depending only on the valency
r
. This ensures that

T
is normal in
G
with a finite number of exceptions, see

[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
. What interests us are those exceptions for
T
. As proposed in
[
12
]
, those exceptions for
T
are worth figuring out. This problem has been solved for
r\leq 5
in several papers, refer to
[
9
,
11
,
12
,
43
]
.
In a recent paper
[
44
]
, the exceptions for
T
are classified under the assumption that a vertex-stabilizer
G_{\alpha}
is solvable. In this paper, we are trying to make some progress on this problem.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected
G
-symmetric Cayley graph of a simple group
T
, where
T\leq G\leq\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
. Assume that
\Gamma
has odd prime valency
r
.
Take an intransitive normal subgroup
K
of
G
, and
define a graph
\Gamma_{G/K}
with
vertex set
V_{K}:=\{\alpha^{K}\mid\alpha\in V\}
and edge set

E_{K}:=\{\{\alpha^{K},\beta^{K}\}\mid\{\alpha,\beta\}\in E\}
. Let

\overline{G}
be the group induced by
G
on
V_{K}
.
Then
\Gamma_{G/K}
is a
\overline{G}
-symmetric graph of valency
r
,

K
is semiregular on
V
and
\overline{G}\cong G/K
, see
[
34
, Theorem 9]
for example. Note that
T\cap K=1
. Then
\overline{G}
has a transitive simple subgroup which is isomorphic to
T
.
These observations suggest us to investigate the symmetric graphs
which have prime valency and admit a transitive simple group.

Let
G^{\infty}
be the solvable residue of
G
, i.e., the last term of the derived series of
G
. Let
\mathbf{rad}(G)
be the solvable
radical of
G
, that is, the maximal solvable normal subgroup of

G
.
In Section
3
, the following result is proved.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected
G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
. Assume that
G
contains a transitive simple subgroup
T
.

(1)

If
G
has a transitive minimal normal subgroup
N
then
either
N
is simple and
T\leq N
, or
N\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
,
r=7
and
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
; for the former case,
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric if
T\neq N
.

(2)

If
T
is not normal in
G
then

G
has a characteristic subgroup
N
such that
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric,
N/\mathbf{rad}(N)
is simple,
|\mathbf{rad}(N)|
is indivisible by
r
,
\mathbf{rad}(N)T\lneq N=N^{\infty}
, and either
\mathbf{rad}(N)
is intransitive on
V
or
G=N\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
.

Part (2) of Theorem
1
provides us a method to determine the normality of symmetric Cayley graphs of prime valency of simple groups, which also suggests us to consider the symmetric graphs satisfying the following
hypothesis.

Hypothesis 1.1
.

\Gamma=(V,E)
is a connected graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
such that

(I)

\Gamma
is
G
-symmetric, where
G
is a nonabelian simple group;

(II)

G=TG_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in V
, where
T
is a simple group with
T\neq G
.

Note that the simple group
T
in (II) is nonabelian; otherwise,

\Gamma
has odd prime order, yielding
r=2
, a contradiction.

Most part of this paper, Sections
4
-
8
, is devoted to analyzing the possibility of the triples

(G,T,G_{\alpha})
satisfying Hypothesis
1.1
. In Section
4
, we give some limitations on
G_{\alpha}
or
G
.
Then the case where
G_{\alpha}
is solvable is dwelt in Section
5
,
the case where
G
(or
T
) is an alternating group or a sporadic simple group is dwelt in Section
6
, and the case where
G
is a classical group is left in Sections
7
and
8
. At last, the following result is proved in Section
9
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected
X
-symmetric Cayley
graph of a finite simple group
T

of prime valency
r\geq 7
, where
T\leq X\leq\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma)
. Assume that
T
is not normal in
X
and

X_{\alpha}
is insolvable, where
\alpha\in V
. Then
T
is isomorphic to a proper subgroup of some composition factor
L
of
X
, and all possible triples
(L,T,r)
are listed in Table
1.1
.

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{|l|l|c|l|l|}\hline\cr\mbox{Line}&L&T&r&\mbox{Conditions}\\
\hline\cr 1&\mathrm{A}_{n}&\mathrm{A}_{n-1}&\geq 7&r\,\,\big|\,\,n,r^{2}\nmid n,n\mbox{ not prime}\\
\hline\cr 2&\mathrm{A}_{r+1}&|T|=r+1&\geq 7&\Gamma=\mathsf{K}_{r+1}\\
\hline\cr 3&\mathrm{U}_{2m}(2^{f})&\mathrm{U}_{2m-1}(2^{f})&2^{mf}+1&m\geq 2,mf=2^{e}>2\\
\hline\cr 4&\Omega_{2m+1}(p^{f})&\mathrm{P\Omega}^{-}_{2m}(p^{f})&{p^{mf}-1\over p^{{m\over d}f}-1}&m\geq 3,mf=d^{e},\mbox{odd primes }p,d\\
\hline\cr 5&\mathrm{P\Omega}^{-}_{10}(2^{f})&\mathrm{U}_{5}(2^{f})&2^{4f}+1&f=2^{e}>1\\
\hline\cr 6&\Omega^{+}_{2m}(2^{f})&\mathrm{Sp}_{2m-2}(2^{f})&{2^{mf}-1\over 2^{mf\over d}-1}&m\geq 4,mf=d^{e},\mbox{prime }d\\
\hline\cr 7&\Omega^{+}_{2m}(2^{f})&\Omega^{-}_{2m-2}(2^{f})&{2^{mf}-1\over 2^{{m\over d}f}-1}&m\geq 5,mf=d^{e},\mbox{odd prime }d\\
\hline\cr 8&\Omega^{+}_{2m}(p^{f})&\mathrm{P\Omega}^{-}_{2m-2}(p^{f})&{p^{mf}-1\over p^{f}-1}&\mbox{odd prime }m\geq 5,f=m^{e},\\
\hline\cr 9&\mathrm{P\Omega}^{+}_{2m}(p^{f})&\Omega_{2m-1}(p^{f})&{p^{mf}-1\over p^{{m\over d}f}-1}&m\geq 5,mf=d^{e},\mbox{odd primes }p,d\\
\hline\cr 10&\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2^{f})&\mathrm{SL}_{2}(2^{2f})&2^{f}+1&f=2^{e}>2\\
\hline\cr 11&\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2^{f})&\mathrm{G}_{2}(2^{2f}),\mathrm{SL}_{2}(2^{3f})&2^{2f}+1&f=2^{e}\geq 2\\
\hline\cr 12&\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(2^{f})&\Omega^{-}_{2m}(2^{f})&{2^{mf}-1\over 2^{{m\over d}f}-1}&m\geq 4,mf=d^{e},\mbox{prime }d\\
\hline\cr 13&\mathrm{L}_{n}(p^{f})&\mathrm{L}_{n-1}(p^{f})&{p^{nf}-1\over p^{{n\over d}f}-1}&n>d,nf=d^{e},\mbox{odd prime }d\\
\hline\cr 14&\mathrm{L}_{n}(p^{f})&\mathrm{L}_{m}(p^{{n\over m}f})&{p^{(n-1)f}-1\over p^{f}-1}&1<m<n,\mbox{ and }(*):\,m\,\,\big|\,\,n,\\
&&&&f=(n-1)^{e},\mbox{odd prime }n-1\\
&&\mathrm{PSp}_{m}(p^{{n\over m}f})&&(*),\mbox{even }m\geq 4,p=2\mbox{ if }m=n\\
&&\mathrm{P\Omega}^{-}_{m}(p^{{n\over m}f})&&(*),\mbox{even }{m\over 2}\geq 4,p=2\mbox{ if }m=n\\
&&\mathrm{G}_{2}(p^{{n\over m}f})&&(*),m=6,p=2\\
\hline\cr 15&\mathrm{U}_{6}(2)&\mathrm{U}_{5}(2)&7&\\
\hline\cr 16&\Omega^{+}_{12}(2)&\mathrm{Sp}_{10}(2)&7&\\
\hline\cr 17&\mathrm{Sp}_{12}(2)&\Omega^{-}_{12}(2)&7&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1.1.
Exceptions for
T
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we collects a few of preliminary results which will be used frequently in the following sections.

2.1.
Primitive divisors

Let
\Phi_{m}(q)
be the
m
-th cyclotomic polynomial evaluated at a prime power
q=p^{f}
. Then

q^{m}-1=\prod_{d\mid m}\Phi_{d}(q),\,\,q^{m}+1=\prod_{d\mid 2m,d\nmid m}\Phi_{d}(q).

Noting that

\prod_{d\mid mf,d\nmid f}\Phi_{d}(p)={q^{m}-1\over q-1}=\prod_{1<d\mid m}\Phi_{d}(q),

the next lemma is easily shown.

Lemma 2.1
.

If
{q^{d}-1\over q-1}
is a prime then
d
is a prime,
(d,q-1)=1
,
f
is a power of
d
, and either
d
is odd or
d=p=2
.

Recall that a primitive prime divisor of
q^{m}-1
is a prime which divides
q^{m}-1
but does not divide
q^{l}-1
for any
0<l<m
. If
r
is a primitive prime divisor of
q^{m}-1
, then

q
has order
m
modulo
r
, and thus
m
is a divisor of
r-1
; if further
r\,\,\big|\,\,(q^{n}-1)
with
n\geq 1
then
m\,\,\big|\,\,n
.
Let
\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)
be the product of all primitive prime divisors of
q^{m}-1
(counting multiplicities). Then the following lemma holds.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)\neq 1
then
every prime divisor of
\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)
is no less than
m+1
, and
(\Phi_{m}^{*}(q),q^{n}-1)\neq 1
yields that
m\,\,\big|\,\,n
.

It is easily shown that
\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)
is a divisor of

\Phi_{m\over d}^{*}(q^{d})
, where
d
is a divisor of
m
. In particular,
\Phi_{mf}^{*}(p)
is a divisor of
\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)
.
Moreover, by Zsigmondy’s Theorem, we have
the following fact.

Lemma 2.3
.

\Phi_{m}^{*}(q)=1
if and only if
(m,q)=(6,2)
or
m=2
and
q=p=2^{s}-1
for some prime
s\geq 2
.

2.2.
Factorizations

All groups in this paper are assumed to be finite, and our group-theoretic notation mostly consistent with
[
31
]
.

Let
G
be a group.
An expression
G=XY
is called a factorization of
G
if
X
and
Y
are proper subgroups of
G
, where
X
and
Y
are called factors. A factorization
G=XY
is called
maximal if both
X
and
Y
are maximal subgroups of
G
.
The next lemma collects a few of simple facts, most of them follow from Lagrange’s Theorem.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
G=XY
be factorization of
G
. Then the following hold.

(1)

YX=G=A^{g}Y^{h}
for
X\leq A\leq G
and
g,h\in G
.

(2)

|G:X|=|Y:(X\cap Y)|
and
|G:Y|=|X:(X\cap Y)|
; in particular,
|X|
and
|Y|
are divisible by
|G:Y|
and
|G:X|
, respectively.

(3)

|G|_{p}\leq|X|_{p}|Y|_{p}
,
|X|_{p}\geq|G:Y|_{p}
and
|Y|_{p}\geq|G:X|_{p}
, where
p
is a prime and
|\cdot|_{p}
means the largest
p
-power dividing
|\cdot|
.

(4)

There is a maximal factorization
G=AB
with
X\leq A
and
Y\leq B
.

Let
X
be a subgroup of a group
G
.
The intersection
\cap_{g\in G}X^{g}
is called the core of
X
in
G
, denoted by
\mathbf{core}_{G}(X)
. If
\mathbf{core}_{G}(X)=1
, i.e.,
X
contains no normal subgroup of
G
other than
1
, then
X
is called a core-free subgroup of
G
.

Given a factorization
G=XY
,
we have a
transitive action of
G
on
[G:Y]:=\{Yg\mid g\}
by right multiplication, where the kernel is
\mathbf{core}_{G}(Y)
. Clearly,
X
acts transitively on
[G:Y]
. If
Y
is core-free in
G
then
G
(and hence
X
) acts faithfully on
[G:Y]
, so all orbits of a normal subgroup of
X
have equal length greater than
1
. This implies the next fact.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
G
be a group and
G=XY
be a factorization. If
Y
is core-free in
G
, then

X\cap Y
is core-free in both
X
and
G
.

A factorization
G=XY
is called core-free if
X
and
Y
are core-free in
G
.
Let
G=XY
be a core-free factorization. Assume that
X<A\leq G
. Then we have a factorization
A=X(A\cap Y)
. By Lemma
2.5
, since
G=AY
, we know that
A\cap Y
is core-free in
A
. In general,
X
is not necessarily core-free in
A
, and the factorization
G=AY
is not necessarily core-free too.

For a simple group
S
and a group
G
, by
S\preccurlyeq G
or
G\succcurlyeq S
we means that

G
has a composition factor (isomorphic to)
S
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
G=XB
be a core-free factorization and
X\leq A\leq G
.
Assume that
N\unlhd A
,
A/N
is almost simple, and
X^{\infty}\not\leq N
. Let
\overline{A}=A/N
,
\overline{X}=XN/N
and
\overline{Y}=(A\cap B)N/N
.
Then either
\overline{A}=\overline{X}\,\overline{Y}
is core-free, or one of the following holds:

(1)

\overline{A}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq X
;

(2)

|G:B|
is a divisor of
|\overline{A}:\overline{A}^{\infty}||N|
.

If
\mathbf{core}_{G}(A)=1
and
N=\mathbf{rad}(A)
normalize
A\cap B

then

\overline{A}^{\infty}\not\preccurlyeq A\cap B
.

Proof.

Assume that either
\overline{X}
or
\overline{Y}
is not core-free in
\overline{A}
.
If
\overline{X}
is not core-free in
\overline{A}
then part (1) of this lemma follows.
Suppose that
\overline{Y}
is not core-free in
\overline{A}
.
Then
NA^{\infty}\leq N(A\cap B)
. Thus
|G:B|=|A:(A\cap B)|=|A:N(A\cap B)||N(A\cap B):(A\cap B)|
, and so
|G:B|
is a divisor of
|A:NA^{\infty}||N|
. Then
part (2) of this lemma occurs.

Now let
\mathbf{core}_{G}(A)=1
and
\mathbf{rad}(A)
normalize
A\cap B
.
Then
((A\cap B)\mathbf{rad}(A))^{\infty}=(A\cap B)^{\infty}
.
Suppose that
S\preccurlyeq A\cap B
.
Then

(A\cap B)\mathbf{rad}(A)\geq A^{\infty}\mathbf{rad}(A)
, and so

A^{\infty}\leq((A\cap B)\mathbf{rad}(A))^{\infty}=(A\cap B)^{\infty}
.
Thus
A^{\infty}\leq B\cap A
, which contradicts Lemma
2.5
. Then the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
G=XY
be a core-free factorization. Suppose that
G
is almost simple.
Then there exists a core-free maximal factorization
G_{0}=A_{0}B_{0}
such that

(1)

G\geq G_{0}=(A_{0}\cap X)(B_{0}\cap Y)
,
G^{\infty}\leq G_{0}
,
X^{\infty}\leq A_{0}
and
Y^{\infty}\leq B_{0}
;

(2)

|X:(A_{0}\cap X)|
and
|Y:(B_{0}\cap Y)|
are divisors of
|G:G_{0}|
.

Proof.

If
G^{\infty}X=G=G^{\infty}Y
, taking maximal subgroups
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
of
G
with
X\leq A_{0}
and
Y\leq B_{0}
, then
G=A_{0}B_{0}
is a desired factorization. Thus, with out loss of generality, we assume next that
G\neq G^{\infty}X
.

Let
G_{1}=G^{\infty}X
.
Then
G=G_{1}Y
, and
G_{1}=X(G_{1}\cap Y)
. Let
Y_{1}=G_{1}\cap Y
. It is easy to see that
X
and
Y_{1}
are core-free in
G_{1}
. Since
G=G_{1}Y
,
by Lemma
2.4
,
|Y:Y_{1}|=|G:G_{1}|
. Moreover,
Y^{\infty}\leq G^{\infty}=G_{1}^{\infty}
, and so
Y^{\infty}\leq G_{1}\cap Y=Y_{1}
, yielding
Y^{\infty}=Y_{1}^{\infty}
.
Noting that
|G_{1}|<|G|
, by induction,
there are
G_{0}\leq G_{1}
and a core-free maximal factorization
G_{0}=A_{0}B_{0}
such that
G_{1}^{\infty}\leq G_{0}
,
X^{\infty}\leq A_{0}

Y_{1}^{\infty}\leq B_{0}
,
|X:(A_{0}\cap X)|
and
|Y_{1}:(B_{0}\cap Y_{1})|
are divisors of
|G_{1}:G_{0}|
. Since
Y_{1}\leq Y
,
we have
|Y:(B_{0}\cap Y)||(B_{0}\cap Y):(B_{0}\cap Y_{1})|=|Y:(B_{0}\cap Y_{1})|=|Y:Y_{1}||Y_{1}:(B_{0}\cap Y_{1})|
, it follows that
|Y:(B_{0}\cap Y)|
is a divisor of
|G:G_{0}|
. Clearly,
G^{\infty}=G_{1}^{\infty}\leq G_{0}
and
Y^{\infty}=Y_{1}^{\infty}\leq B_{0}
. Then the factorization
G_{0}=A_{0}B_{0}
is desired. ∎

2.3.
Stabilizers

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
. Denote by
\mathbf{soc}(G)
the
socle of
G
,
and by
\pi(G)
the set of prime divisors of
|G|
.
By

[
6
, Lemma 1.1]
,
[
34
, Theorem 9]
and

[
35
, Theorem 1]
, the following lemma holds.

Lemma 2.8
.

|G_{\alpha}|_{r}=r=\max\pi(G_{\alpha})
.
If
N\unlhd G
then either
N
is semiregular on
V
, or
N_{\alpha}
is transitive on
\Gamma(\alpha)

and
N
has at most two orbits on
V
.

Take
\{\alpha,\beta\}\in E
. Then the arc-stabilizer
G_{\alpha\beta}
is a Hall
r^{\prime}
-subgroup of
G_{\alpha}
. Since
\Gamma
is
G
-symmetric,

(\alpha,\beta)^{x}=(\beta,\alpha)
for some
x\in G
.
Clearly, such an
x
has even order and lies in the normalizer
\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
of
G_{\alpha\beta}
. Thus the element
x
may be chosen as a
2
-element. Moreover, since
\Gamma
is connected,
\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle=G
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\{\alpha,\beta\}\in E
. Then
\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle=G
for some
2
-element
x\in\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
x^{2}\in G_{\alpha\beta}
.

Note that
G_{\alpha}
induces a transitive permutation group say
G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}
(on

\Gamma(\alpha)
) of degree
r
. Then
G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}
and
r
are known as in Table
2.2
, refer to

[
7
, pp. 99]
. Moreover, if
r={p^{fd}-1\over p^{f}-1}
for some prime
p
then, by Lemma
2.1
,
d
is a prime and
f
is a power of
d
.

\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})&\mathbb{Z}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{23}\\
\hline\cr r&r&r\geq 5&{q^{d}-1\over q-1}&11&11&23\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 2.2.
Transitive groups of prime degree.

Let
G_{\alpha}^{[1]}
be the kernel of
the action of
G_{\alpha}
on
\Gamma(\alpha)
. Then
G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}\cong G_{\alpha}/G_{\alpha}^{[1]}
.
Take
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, and set
G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}=G_{\alpha}^{[1]}\cap G_{\beta}^{[1]}
. Then

G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}
has order a prime power, see
[
14
]
.
For
r=3
, by
[
8
,
45
,
46
]
,
G_{\alpha}
and
G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}
are listed in
Table
2.3
.

\small\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr G_{\alpha}&\mathbb{Z}_{3}&\mathrm{S}_{3}&\mathrm{D}_{12}&\mathrm{S}_{4}&\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr G_{\alpha}^{[1]}&1&1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}\\
\hline\cr G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}&1&1&1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}\\
\hline\cr G_{\alpha\beta}&1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}&\mathrm{D}_{8}&\mathrm{D}_{8}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 2.3.
Stabilizers of symmetric cubic graphs.

In general, we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\alpha\beta}/G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}\lesssim(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})_{\beta}\times(G_{\beta}^{\Gamma(\beta)})_{\alpha},\,\,(G_{\alpha}^{[1]})^{\Gamma(\beta)}\unlhd(G_{\beta}^{\Gamma(\beta)})_{\alpha}\cong(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})_{\beta}\\
G_{\alpha}^{[1]}=G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}.(G_{\alpha}^{[1]})^{\Gamma(\beta)},\,\,G_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}.(G_{\alpha}^{[1]})^{\Gamma(\beta)}).G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}.\end{split}

Then, combining with
[
15
,
47
]
, we have the following result.

Lemma 2.10
.

(1)

G_{\alpha}
is soluble if and only if

G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}\leq\mathrm{AGL}_{1}(r)
.

(2)

Either
\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)\unlhd G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}
and
r={q^{d}-1\over q-1}
, or
G_{\alpha}
is listed in Table
2.4
.

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)}&\mathbb{Z}_{r}&\mathrm{S}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{23}\\
\hline\cr G_{\alpha}&(\mathbb{Z}_{l^{\prime}}{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{r}){.}\mathbb{Z}_{l}&\mathrm{S}_{r},\mathrm{S}_{r-1}{\times}\mathrm{S}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{23}\\
&l^{\prime}\,\,\big|\,\,l\,\,\big|\,\,r-1&(\mathrm{A}_{r-1}{\times}\mathrm{A}_{r}){.}2&\mathrm{A}_{r-1}{\times}\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{5}{\times}\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{M}_{10}{\times}\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{22}{\times\mathrm{M}_{23}}\\
&&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}{\times}\mathrm{S}_{5}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}{\times}\mathrm{A}_{5}&&\mathrm{A}_{6}{\times}\mathrm{M}_{11}&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 2.4.
Some stabilizers with
G_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}=1
.

3.
The proof of Theorem
1

In this section,
\Gamma=(V,E)
is a connected
graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
. Then

G
has at most one transitive minimal normal subgroup.

Proof.

Suppose that
G
has two distinct transitive minimal normal subgroups, say
M
and
N
.
Then
M\cap N=1
, and so
M
and
N
centralize each other. Thus

M
and
N
are nonabelian and regular on
V
, refer to

[
7
, pp.108-109, Lemma 4.2A and Theorem 4.2A]
.
Clearly,
X:=MN
is not regular on
V
. By Lemma
2.8
,
\Gamma
is
X
-symmetric; in particular,
r\in\pi(X_{\alpha})
for
\alpha\in V
. Since
NX_{\alpha}=X=MX_{\alpha}
, we have
M\cong X/N\cong X_{\alpha}\cong X/M\cong N
. Then
|M|=|N|
is divisible by
r
but not by
r^{2}
. Noting that
M
and
N
are direct products of isomorphic simple groups, it follows that
M\cong T\cong N
for some nonabelian simple group
T
. Then the actions of
N
and
M

on
V
are equivalent to the actions of
T
on
T
by right and left multiplications.
Identifying
V
with
T
and letting
\alpha=1\in T
,
the neighborhood
\Gamma(\alpha)
is in fact a conjugacy class of
T
with length
r
,
which is impossible by
[
22
, pp.36, Theorem 3.7]
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
.
Assume that
G
has a transitive simple subgroup
T
.
If
T
is normal in a characteristic subgroup of
G
then
T
is normal in
G
.

Proof.

Assume
N
is a characteristic subgroup of
G
, and
T\unlhd N
.
Then
T^{g}\unlhd N
for each
g\in G
.
Suppose that
T\neq T^{g}
for some
g
.
Then
N
is not regular on
V
, and so
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric.
Noting that
T\cap T^{g}=1
, it follows that
T
and
T^{g}
centralize each other, and hence they are regular on
V
. Thus
N
has two transitive minimal normal subgroups, which contradicts Lemma
3.1
. Then the corollary follows.
∎

Recall that a permutation group

G
is quasiprimitive if its minimal normal subgroups are all transitive.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
, and let
N=\mathbf{soc}(G)
.
Assume that
G
is quasiprimitive on
V
and
G
has a transitive simple subgroup
T
.
Then either
r=7
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
and
G\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
, or
N
is simple with
T\leq N
. Moreover, for the latter case, if
T\neq N
then
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
,
N
is the unique minimal normal subgroup of
G
. Since
\Gamma
has odd valency,
|V|
is even, and then
T
is a nonabelian simple group.

Assume first that
N
is abelian. Then
N\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
and
G\lesssim\mathrm{AGL}_{d}(p)
for
some prime
p
and integer
d\geq 1
. In this case,
T\lesssim\mathrm{GL}_{d}(p)
, and
N
is regular on
V
.
Since
\Gamma
has odd valency,
|N|=|V|
is even, and so

p=2
. Since
T
is transitive on
V
, we have
|T:T_{\alpha}|=2^{d}
.
By
[
19
]
,
d\geq 3
and either
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{2^{d}}
or
\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
with
{q^{n}-1\over q-1}=2^{d}
and
n
a prime. Note that
\mathrm{A}_{2^{d}}\not\lesssim\mathrm{GL}_{d}(2)
, see
[
25
, pp. 186, Proposition 5.3.7]
. Then
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
and
{q^{n}-1\over q-1}=2^{d}
. By Lemma
2.3
,
n=2
and
q=2^{d}-1
is a prime.
By
[
25
, pp. 188, Theorem 5.3.9]
, we have
d\geq{q-1\over(2,q-1)}=2^{d-1}-1
, yielding
d=3
. Then
N\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
,
|V|=8

r=7
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
and
G\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
.

Next let
N
be nonabelian. Write
N=T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{k}
for integer
k\geq 1
and isomorphic nonabelian simple groups
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
.
Suppose that
T\not\leq N
. Then
T\cap N=1
, and
TN/N\cong T
. Since
N
is the unique minimal normal subgroup of
G
, we have

\mathbf{C}_{G}(N)=1
, and thus
TN\cong TN/\mathbf{C}_{TN}(N)\lesssim\mathbf{Aut}(N)
. It follows that
T\cong TN/N\lesssim\mathbf{Out}(N)
, the outer automorphism group of
N
.
Noting that
\mathbf{Out}(N)\cong(\mathbf{Out}(T_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathbf{Out}(T_{k})){:}\mathrm{S}_{k}
, it follows that

T\lesssim\mathrm{S}_{k}
. In particular,

|T|
is a divisor of
k!
.
Since
N
is transitive on
V
, we have
G=NG_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in V
, and so

T\cong TN/N\leq G/N\cong G_{\alpha}/(G_{\alpha}\cap N)
.
Then
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable, and so
\Gamma
is

(G,2)
-arc-transitive. By
[
38
, Theorem 2]
,

G
satisfies III(b)(i) or III(c) given in

[
38
, pp.229]
. It follows that there is a prime
divisor
p
of
|N|
such that
|V|
is divisible by
p^{k}
. Since

T
is transitive on
V
, it follows that
p^{k}
is a divisor of

|T|
. Then
k!
is divisible by
p^{k}
, and so
k\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lfloor{k\over p^{i}}\rfloor
, which is impossible.
Therefore,
T\leq N
.

Assume that
T\neq N
. Then
N
is not regular on
V
, and so
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric by Lemma
2.8
. Suppose that
k>1
.
Then, by Lemma
3.1
, every
T_{i}
is intransitive on
V
.
Noting that
\Gamma
is not bipartite, by Lemma
2.8
,

|T_{i}|
is a proper divisor of
|V|
, and so
|T_{i}|
is a proper divisor of
|T|
as
T
is transitive
on
V
, where
1\leq i\leq k
. Now consider the
projections
\phi_{i}:N\rightarrow T_{i},x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\mapsto x_{i}
,
where
x_{j}\in T_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq k
, and
1\leq i\leq k
. Then

\phi_{i}(T)\neq 1
for some
i
, and thus
T\lesssim T_{i}
, which is impossible. Thus
k=1
, and
N
is simple. This completes the proof. ∎

The following corollary completes the proof of part (1) of Theorem
1
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
.
Assume that
G
has a transitive minimal normal subgroup
N
and a transitive simple subgroup
T
. Then either
r=7
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
and
G\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
, or
T\leq N
and
N
is simple.

Proof.

Choose a maximal intransitive normal subgroup
M
of
G
. Then
T\cap M=N\cap M=1
;
in particular,
MN=M\times N
. If
M=1
then
G
is quasiprimitive
on
V
, and so the corollary holds by Lemma
3.2
. Thus
let
M\neq 1
. This implies that
N
is nonabelian as
M\leq\mathbf{C}_{G}(N)\neq N
. Write
N=T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{k}
for integer

k\geq 1
and isomorphic nonabelian simple groups
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
.
Then
G
acts transitively on
\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}\}
by conjugation.
Let
\overline{G}=G/M
and
\overline{T_{i}}=T_{i}M/M
for
1\leq i\leq k
. It is easily shown that
\overline{G}
acts transitively on

\{\overline{T_{1}},\ldots,\overline{T_{k}}\}
by conjugation.
Let
\overline{N}=NM/M
.
Then
\overline{N}
is a minimal normal subgroup of

\overline{G}
. Let
\overline{T}=TM/M
.
Note that

\overline{N}\cong N
and
\overline{T}\cong T
.

Since
\Gamma
is not bipartite,
M
has at least three orbits
on
V
. By Lemma
2.8
,
|M|
is a proper divisor of
|V|
, and so
|M|
is a proper divisor of
|T|
.
Identify
\overline{G}
with a subgroup of
\mathbf{Aut}(\Gamma_{G/M})
.
Then Lemma
3.2
works for
\Gamma_{G/M}
.
We have that
\overline{T}\leq\overline{N}
and
\overline{N}
is simple.
This yields that
T\leq TM\leq NM
. Suppose that
T\not\leq N
. Then

N\cap T=1
, and so
T\cong TN/N\leq MN/N\cong M
. In particular,

|T|
is a divisor of
|M|
, a contradiction. Therefore,
N\geq T
, and the
corollary follows. ∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of odd prime valency
r
. Let
K
be a maximal intransitive normal subgroup of
G
.
Assume that
G
has a transitive simple subgroup
T
. Then either
K=1
, or
T\cong TK/K\leq\mathbf{soc}(G/K)
.

Proof.

Considering
\Gamma_{G/K}
,
TK/K
and
G/K
, by Lemma
3.2
,
either the lemma holds, or
\mathbf{soc}(G/K)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
.
Assume that the latter case occurs. Then

G/K\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
,

T\cong TK/K\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
and
r=7
. We next show
K=1
.

Let
\mathbf{soc}(G/K)=N/K
, where
N\unlhd G
. Then
N
is a regular subgroup of
G
.
Noting that
|N|=|V|
, since
T
is transitive on
V
, we know that
|V|
is a divisor of
|T|=168
. It follows that
|K|
is a divisor of
21
. Thus
K
is a subgroup of the cyclic group or the Frobenius group of order
21
. In particular,
|\mathbf{Aut}(K)|
is indivisible by
8
.
Take a Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
of
N
and consider the conjugation of
P
on
K
. Noting that
P\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
, it follows that

\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)\geq\mathbf{C}_{P}(K)\neq 1
. Clearly,
\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)\unlhd G
.
Then
G/K
has a (nontrivial) normal subgroup
\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)K/K
.
Recalling that
N/K
is
the unique minimal normal subgroup of
G/K
, we have
N/K=\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)K/K
, yielding
N=\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)K
. It follows that
P\leq\mathbf{C}_{N}(K)
and
N=K\times P
.

Suppose that
K\neq 1
. Then we may choose a normal Hall subgroup
H
of
N
such that
P\leq H
and
|N:H|\in\{3,7\}
.
Note that
H
is a characteristic subgroup of
N
. This implies that
H\unlhd G
. Then we get a symmetric graph
\Gamma_{G/H}
of valency
7
and order
3
or
7
, which is impossible. Thus
K=1
, and the lemma follows.
∎

Now we are ready to prove part (2) of Theorem
1
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,E)
be a connected

G
-symmetric graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
.
Assume that
G
has a transitive simple subgroup
T
, and
T
is not normal in
G
. Then either
G\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
, or
G
has
a characteristic subgroup
N
such that

\mathbf{rad}(N)
is intransitive on
V
,
N/\mathbf{rad}(N)
is simple,
r\not\in\pi(\mathbf{rad}(N))
and

\mathbf{rad}(N)T\lneq N=N^{\infty}
.

Proof.

We choose a minimal member
N
from those characteristic subgroups of
G
which
contain
T
. Note that
T\leq N^{\infty}
, and so
N=N^{\infty}
by the choice of
N
.
Then
T
is not normal in
N
by Corollary
3.1
, and
\Gamma
is
N
-symmetric by Lemma
2.8
.

Assume first that
N
is quasiprimitive on
V
. Then, by the choice of
N
and Lemma
3.2
, either
N
is simple or
N\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
. For the latter, noting that
\mathbf{rad}(N)
is normal in
G
, we have
G\cong\mathrm{AGL}_{3}(2)
by Corollary
3.2
. Then the lemma is true.

Assume that
N
is not quasiprimitive on
V
.
Let
K
be a maximal intransitive normal subgroup of
N
. Then
K\neq 1
and, by Lemma
3.2
and Corollary
3.3
,
T\cong TK/K\leq\mathbf{soc}(N/K)
and
\mathbf{soc}(N/K)
is simple.
Recalling that
N=N^{\infty}
, it is easily shown that
\mathbf{soc}(N/K)=N/K
.
By the choice of
K
and Lemma
2.8
,
|K|
is a proper divisor of
|V|
, and thus
|K|
is a proper divisor of
|T|
.

For
\sigma\in\mathbf{Aut}(G)
, we have
K^{\sigma}\unlhd N^{\sigma}=N
, and so
K^{\sigma}K/K\unlhd N/K
. If
K^{\sigma}\neq K
then
T\cong TK/K\leq N/K=K^{\sigma}K/K\cong K^{\sigma}/(K^{\sigma}\cap K)
, and so
|T|
is a divisor of
|K|
, a contradiction. Therefore,
K^{\sigma}=K
. It follows that
K
is characteristic in
G
.

Set
X=KT
and let
\alpha\in V
. Noting
K\cap T=1
and
KT=X=TX_{\alpha}
, we have
|K|=|X:T|=|X_{\alpha}:T_{\alpha}|
.
Take two characteristic subgroups
I
and
J
of
K

with
I\leq J
. It is easy to see that both
I
and
J
are characteristic in
G
. Then
JT/I=(J/I)(TI/I)
, and
\mathbf{C}_{N/I}(J/I)
is characteristic in
G/I
. Set
C/I=\mathbf{C}_{N/I}(J/I)
. Then
C
is characteristic in
G
. Choose
J
and
I
such
J/I
is a direct product of isomorphic simple groups, and write
J/I=S_{1}\times\cdots\times S_{l}
, where
S_{i}
are simple.

Suppose that
K
is insolvable. Then we may choose
J
and
I
such that
S_{1}\cong\cdots\cong S_{l}\cong S
for some nonabelian simple group
S
.
In this case,
(C/I)\cap(J/I)=1
, and then
C/I
is properly contained in
N/I
. By the choice of
N
, we have
l>1
and

TI/I\not\leq C/I
; otherwise, we have
N/I=C/I
, yielding that
J/I
is abelian, a contradiction. Then
T\cong TI/I\cong(TI/I)(C/I)/(C/I)\lesssim\mathbf{Aut}(J/I)\cong\mathbf{Aut}(S)\wr\mathrm{S}_{l}
. Recalling that
|K|
is a proper divisor of
|T|
, it follows that
|S|
is a proper divisor of
|T|
, and thus we have
T\lesssim\mathrm{S}_{l}
.
Let
p
be an odd prime of
|S|
. Then we have
p^{l}\leq|K|_{p}\leq|T|_{p}\leq|\mathrm{S}_{l}|_{p}
, yielding
l\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lfloor{l\over p^{i}}\rfloor
, which is impossible.
Thus
K
is solvable, and so
K=\mathbf{rad}(N)
.

Suppose that
r\in\pi(K)
.
Then we may choose
J
and
I
such that
S_{1}\cong\cdots\cong S_{l}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{r}
. Recalling that
|K|=|X_{\alpha}:T_{\alpha}|
, we have
r^{l}\leq|K|_{r}=|X_{\alpha}:T_{\alpha}|_{r}
.
This yields that
\Gamma
is
X
-symmetric as
r\in\pi(X_{\alpha})
, and so
|X_{\alpha}|_{r}=r
by Lemma
2.8
. Then
|K|_{r}=r
, and so
l=1
and
J/I\cong\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.
Thus
JT/I=(J/I)(TI/I)=(J/I)\times(TI/I)
, and then
TI/I\leq C/I
. By the choice of
N
, we have
C=N
and
N/I=\mathbf{C}_{N/I}(J/I)
. This forces that
\mathbf{Z}(N/I)\geq J/I\cong\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.
Since
|K|_{r}=r
, we know that
J/I
is a Sylow
r
-subgroup group
of
K/I
. Take a Sylow
r
-subgroup
Q/I
of
TI/I
. Then
(J/I)\times(Q/I)
is a Sylow
r
-subgroup of
X/I
and has order
r|T|_{r}
. By Lemma
2.8
,
|N_{\alpha}|_{r}=r
, since
N=TN_{\alpha}
, we have
|N|_{r}=r|T|_{r}
. It follows that
(J/I)\times(Q/I)
is a Sylow
r
-subgroup of
N/I
. By Gaschütz’ Theorem (refer to
[
1
, pp. 31, (10.4)]
),
N/I
splits over
J/I
.
Let
M/I=(N/I)^{\prime}
. Then
T\leq M
,
M
is characteristic in
G
, and

|M|_{r}=|T|_{r}<r|T|_{r}=|N_{r}|
, which contradicts the choice of
N
.
Therefore,
|K|_{r}=1
.

Finally, if
TK=N
then
r|T|_{r}=|T|_{r}|N_{\alpha}|_{r}=|N|_{r}=|T|_{r}|K|_{r}
, yielding
|K|_{r}=r
, a contradiction. Thus
TK\neq N
, and the lemma follows.
∎

4.
Some limitations on the vertex-stabilizers

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\Gamma
,
G
,
T
and
r
be as in Hypothesis
1.1
. Then

G
is not an exceptional simple group of Lie type, and

G_{\alpha}
has at most one insolvable composition factor.

Proof.

Suppose that
G_{\alpha}
has at least two insolvable composition factors. By (
2.1
) and Lemma
2.10
,
G_{\alpha}
has exactly two insolvable composition factors, one is
S=\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})
and the other one, say
S_{1}
, is a composition factor of
(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})_{\beta}
for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
.
We list them as follows.

\small\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|l|l|l}S&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{23}\\
\hline\cr S_{1}&\mathrm{A}_{r-1}&\mathrm{L}_{d-1}(q)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathrm{A}_{6}&\mathrm{M}_{22}\end{array}

Checking the factorizations given in
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
,
we conclude that there is no a simple group
T
such that
G=TG_{\alpha}
,
a contradiction. Then
G_{\alpha}
has at most one insolvable composition factor. Noting the factorization

G=TG_{\alpha}
, our lemma follows from
[
21
, Theorem 1]
. ∎

In view of Lemma
4.1
,
we make some assumptions for the following sections.

Hypothesis 4.1
.

\Gamma=(V,E)
is a connected graph of prime valency
r\geq 3
such that

(I)

\Gamma
is
G
-symmetric, where
G
is a nonabelian simple group;

(II)

G=TG_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in V
, where
T
is a nonabelian simple group with
T\neq G
;

(III)

G
is not an exceptional simple group of Lie type, and
G_{\alpha}
has at most one insolvable composition factor.

By
[
41
]
, (
2.1
) and Lemmas
2.1
,
2.10
and
4.1
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that Hypothesis
4.1
holds, and
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable. Let
S=\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})
. Then
\pi(G_{\alpha})=\pi(S)
, and one of the following holds.

(1)

(S,G_{\alpha},r)
is one of
(\mathrm{A}_{r},\mathrm{A}_{r},r)
,
(\mathrm{A}_{r},\mathrm{S}_{r},r)
,

(\mathrm{L}_{2}(11),\mathrm{L}_{2}(11),11)
,
(\mathrm{M}_{11},\mathrm{M}_{11},11)
and
(\mathrm{M}_{23},\mathrm{M}_{23},23)
.

(2)

S\cong\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)

and

(r,|\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})|,d,q)
is listed as in Table
4.5
. In this case, if

\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})=1
then
G_{\alpha}\lesssim\mathrm{P\Gamma L}_{d}(q)
, if

\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})\neq 1
then

|G_{\alpha}:\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})|
is a divisor of
lf^{2}|\mathrm{GL}_{d}(q)|
, where
l=1
or
(l,d,q)
is one of
(6,3,2)
and
(24,3,3)
.

\small\begin{array}[]{|c|c|l|l|}\hline\cr r&\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})&d&q=p^{f}\\
\hline\cr q+1&1,\,q,\,q^{2},\,q^{3}&2&p=2,f={2^{t}}\\
\hline\cr{q^{d}-1\over q-1}&1,\,q^{d-1},\,q^{d(d-1)\over 2}&\mbox{prime }d\geq 5&f=d^{t}\\
\hline\cr{q^{d}-1\over q-1}&1,\,q^{d-1},\,q^{d}&\mbox{prime }d\geq 3&f=d^{t}\\
\hline\cr 2^{d}-1&1,\,2^{d-1},\,2^{d+1}&\mbox{prime }d\geq 3&q=2\\
\hline\cr q^{2}+q+1&1,\,q^{2},\,q^{6}&3&q\in\{2,3\}\\
\hline\cr 7&1,\,4,\,2^{20}&3&q=2\\
\hline\cr 13&1,\,9,\,3^{6}&3&q=3\\
\hline\cr 31&1,\,2^{4},\,2^{30}&5&q=2\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 4.5.

Remark 4.1
.

Given Hypothesis
4.1
, we
consider the (faithful) transitive action of

G_{\alpha}
on
[G:T]
by right multiplication. Let
n=|G:T|
.
Since
G
is simple, we view
G
as a transitive subgroup of the alternating group
\mathrm{A}_{n}
, and thus we may let
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}\cap G
.
Then there is a general construction based on the coset graphs
for connected graphs satisfying the above (I) and (II), shown below.
Let
H
be a
group which is a possible vertex-stabilizer of some
connected symmetric graph of valency
r
.
For any given faithful transitive permutation representation of
H
on
\Delta=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
and a Hall
r^{\prime}
-subgroup
K
of
H
,
let
\overline{H}
and
\overline{K}
be the images of
H
and
K
respectively.
Determine the normalizer
\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(\overline{K})
of
\overline{K}
in
\mathrm{A}_{n}
, and then seek the elements
x
in
\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(\overline{K})\setminus\overline{H}
which have the property that
x^{2}\in\overline{K}
and
\langle x,\overline{H}\rangle
is a simple group. Note that the simplicity of
\langle x,\overline{H}\rangle
ensures that
\overline{H}\leq\mathrm{A}_{n}
.
Suppose that there is such an
x
, and let
\overline{G}=\langle x,\overline{H}\rangle
.
Then
\overline{G}
has a factorization
\overline{G}=(\mathrm{A}_{n-1}\cap\overline{G})\overline{H}
.
If further
\mathrm{A}_{n-1}\cap\overline{G}
is simple then the coset graph
\mathbf{Cos}(\overline{G},\overline{H},x)
is a desired graph.
In view of this, we sometimes deal with
G
and
G_{\alpha}
as two transitive subgroups of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.

∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\Gamma
,
G
,
T
and
r
be as in Hypothesis
4.1
. Let
n=|G:T|
and view
G
as a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
acting on
\Delta=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
Then either
G_{\alpha}
is imprimitive on
\Delta
, or one of following holds.

(1)

G
,
T
and
G_{\alpha}
are listed in Table
4.6
up to isomorphism.

(2)

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
, and
G_{\alpha}
is almost simple but not
2
-transitive on
\Delta
.

(3)

n=r\geq 7
,
G=\mathrm{A}_{r}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{r-1}
,
|G:T|_{r}=r
and
G_{\alpha}=\mathbb{Z}_{r}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{l}
, where
l
is a divisor of
r-1\over 2
with
(r,l)\neq(7,3)
.

\tiny\begin{array}[]{|l|l|c|c|c|l|l|}\hline\cr\mbox{Line}&r&G_{\alpha}&G&T&|G:T|_{r}&\Gamma\\
\hline\cr 1&11&\mathrm{A}_{11}&\mathrm{A}_{12}&\mathrm{M}_{12}&1&\mathsf{K}_{12}\\
\hline\cr 2&23&\mathrm{A}_{23}&\mathrm{A}_{24}&\mathrm{M}_{24}&1&\mathsf{K}_{24}\\
\hline\cr 3&r=2^{2d-1}\pm 2^{d-1}-1,d\geq 3&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{r+1}&\mathrm{Sp}_{2d}(2)&1&\mathsf{K}_{r+1}\\
\hline\cr 4&r\not\in\{7,11,17,23\}&\mathrm{A}_{r}&\mathrm{A}_{r+1}&\mathrm{L}_{2}(r)&1&\mathsf{K}_{r+1}\\
\hline\cr 5&11&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{12}&\mathrm{M}_{11}&1&\mathsf{K}_{12}\\
\hline\cr 6&11&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{A}_{12}&\mathrm{A}_{11}&1&\mbox{unique}\\
\hline\cr 7&11&\mathbb{Z}_{11}&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&11&\mbox{Cayley}\\
\hline\cr 8&11&\mathbb{Z}_{11}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{5}&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&11&\mathsf{K}_{12}\\
\hline\cr 9&23&\mathbb{Z}_{23}&\mathrm{M}_{23}&\mathrm{M}_{22}&23&\mbox{Cayley}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 4.6
.

Proof.

Suppose that
G_{\alpha}
is primitive on
\Delta
.
Since
G_{\alpha}
has at most one insolvable composition factor,
G_{\alpha}
is almost simple or of affine type.

Case 1
. Assume that
G_{\alpha}
is almost simple.
Since
G
is primitive on
\Delta
with a simple point-stabilizer
T
, either
G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
or
(G,G_{\alpha})
can be read out from
[
30
, Tables III-VI]
by
[
37
, Proposition 6.1]
.
Suppose that
G\neq\mathrm{A}_{n}
. Then, combining Lemma
4.2
, either
(G,T,G_{\alpha})
is desired as
at Lines 1-5 of Table
4.6
and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
, or
r=11
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{12}
and
T\cong\mathrm{M}_{11}
. The latter is excluded by Lemma
2.9
and calculation of
\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
, where
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
.

Now let
G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
. Then
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
by the assumption in this lemma.
We assume that
G_{\alpha}
is
2
-transitive on
\Delta
, and
check
2
-transitive subgroups of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
, refer to
[
4
, pp. 197, Table 7.3]
.
Combining Lemma
4.2
,
n
,
\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha})
and
r
are listed as follows:

\tiny\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|l}n&\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha})&r&\\
\hline\cr 6,5&\mathrm{L}_{2}(4)&5&\\
\hline\cr 8,7&\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)&7&G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)\\
\hline\cr 12,11&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&11&G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)\\
\hline\cr 12,11&\mathrm{M}_{11}&11&G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{M}_{11}\\
\hline\cr 23&\mathrm{M}_{23}&23&G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{M}_{23}\\
\hline\cr 15&\mathrm{A}_{7}&7&G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{A}_{7}\\
\hline\cr{q^{d}-1\over q-1}&\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)&{q^{d}-1\over q-1}>7&d\mbox{ a prime}\\
\end{array}

For all lines but the last one, since
\Gamma
has odd valency,
|V|=|G:G_{\alpha}|
is even, we have
(n,r)\neq(5,5)
,
(7,7)
.
By calculation using GAP
[
40
]
, we conclude that either
(n,r)=(6,5)
or
n=12
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
. Noting that
\mathrm{A}_{5}\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(5)
, the former case is included at Line 4 of Table
4.6
. The latter case implies Line 6 of Table
4.6
.

We suppose next that
n=r={q^{d}-1\over q-1}>7
and
\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha})=\mathrm{L}_{d}(q)
, and deduce a contradiction.
Let
q=p^{f}
for some prime
p
, and let
\sigma
be the Frobenius automorphism of the field of order
q
. Note that, by Lemma
2.1
,
d
is a prime,
(d,q-1)=1
and
f
is a power of
d
.
Thus, by Lemma
2.10
, we may let
G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{l}\rangle\leq\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{d}(q)\cap\mathrm{A}_{n}
, where
l
is a divisor of
f
.
Without loss of generality, we identify
\Delta
with the point-set of the projective geometry
\mathrm{PG}_{d-1}(q)
, and let
G_{\alpha\beta}=q^{d-1}{:}\mathrm{GL}_{d-1}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{l}\rangle
.
Fix
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, and choose a
2
-element
x\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
(\alpha,\beta)^{x}=(\beta,\alpha)
and
\mathrm{A}_{n}=\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle
.

Suppose that
d=2
, in this case,
p=2
and
f=2^{s}
for some
s\geq 2
. Then
G_{\alpha\beta}
is the stabilizer of some projective point
i
, and thus

x
fixes
i
. Then

G_{\alpha\beta}\langle x\rangle
is a solvable
2
-transitive group of degree

q
. It follows that
G_{\alpha\beta}\langle x\rangle\leq\mathrm{A\Gamma L}_{1}(q)=\mathrm{AGL}_{1}(q){:}\langle\sigma\rangle
. Thus
\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle\leq\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q){:}\langle\sigma\rangle
, yielding
\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle\neq\mathrm{A}_{n}=G
, a contradiction.

Now
d
is an odd prime, and so
f
is odd. Then
G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{l}\rangle\leq\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{d}(q)\leq\mathrm{A}_{n}
, and
\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(G_{\alpha\beta})
has a subgroup
P{:}K\cong\mathrm{A\Gamma L}_{d-1}(q)
, where
P=\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha\beta})
and
K\cong\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{d-1}(q)
with
G_{\alpha\beta}\leq PK
.
Let
C=\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(P)
and
N=\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(P)
. Then
P\leq C
,
C\cap K=1
,
N\geq CK=C{:}K
, and so
K\lesssim N/C\lesssim\mathbf{Aut}(P)=\mathrm{GL}_{(d-1)f}(p)
.
Noting that
\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(G_{\alpha\beta})\leq N
, we have

\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(G_{\alpha\beta})=\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})
. Then
K\cong PKC/C\leq\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(G_{\alpha\beta})C/C=\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})C/C\leq\mathbf{N}_{N/C}(G_{\alpha\beta}C/C)\lesssim\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{GL}_{(d-1)f}(p)}(\mathrm{GL}_{d-1}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{l}\rangle)\cong\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{d-1}(q)\cong K
. It follows
C{:}K=\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})C\geq\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})
. Write
G_{\alpha\beta}=P{:}H
with
H\leq K
.
Recalling that
K\leq\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})\leq C{:}K
, we have
\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta})=(C\cap\mathbf{N}_{N}(G_{\alpha\beta}){:}K=P(C\cap H)K=PK
.
Then
x=yz\sigma^{t}
, where
y\in P
,
t
is a divisor of
f
and
z
is contained in a subgroup of
H
isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{d-1}(q)
.
Since
f
is odd, the order of
x

is not a
2
-power unless
t=f
. Then
x=yz\in G_{\alpha\beta}
, a contradiction.

Case 2
.
Suppose that
G_{\alpha}
is not almost simple.
Then
4<n=p^{m}
for some prime
p
and integer
m\geq 1
, and

G_{\alpha}\leq\mathrm{AGL}_{m}(p)
. By
[
37
, Proposition 6.2]
,
one of the following holds:
G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
; or
n=p=r=11
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{11}\lesssim G_{\alpha}\lesssim\mathbb{Z}_{11}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{5}
; or
n=p=r=23
,
G=\mathrm{M}_{23}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{22}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{23}\lesssim G_{\alpha}\lesssim\mathbb{Z}_{23}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{11}
. Calculation shows that the last two case give Lines 7-9 of Table
4.6
.

Assume that
G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
. Then
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
. If

G_{\alpha}
is solvable, then
n=r=p
and
G_{\alpha}=\mathbb{Z}_{r}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{l}
, where
l
is a divisor of
r-1\over 2
. In this case, by calculation, we exclude the case
(r,l)=(7,3)
, and part (3) of this lemma follows.
Thus we assume further that
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable.
In particular,
m>1
, and
n\geq 8
.

Clearly,
\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha})\leq\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})\neq 1
, and so
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha}))\neq 1
.
It follows that
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha}))
is a regular normal subgroup of

G_{\alpha}
(acting on
\Delta
), and thus

\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha})=\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha}))
.
Then, by Lemma
2.10
, we may let

G_{\alpha}=\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha}){:}((\mathbb{Z}_{l}\times\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q)){:}\langle\sigma^{e}\rangle)
and
r={q^{d}-1\over q-1}
, where

l
is a divisor of
q-1
,
\sigma
is the Frobenius automorphism of the field of order
q
, and
q
is a power of the prime
p
.
(Recall that, since
r
is a prime,
d
is a prime and
(d,q-1)=1
.)
Replacing
T
by its a conjugation in
G
, we let
(\mathbb{Z}_{l}\times\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q)){:}\langle\sigma^{e}\rangle\leq T
. Then
\Gamma
is
T
-symmetric
and
T_{\alpha}=(\mathbb{Z}_{l}\times\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q)){:}\langle\sigma^{e}\rangle
, and thus

T_{\alpha}^{[1]}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{l}
and
T_{\alpha\beta}^{[1]}=1
, where
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
. By (
2.1
), it is easily shown that
l=1
. Then we have
G_{\alpha}=\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha}){:}(\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{e}\rangle)
. It follows that

p^{m}=|\mathbf{O}_{p}(G_{\alpha})|=q^{d}
, and then
\mathrm{ASL}_{d}(q)\leq G_{\alpha}\leq\mathrm{A\Gamma L}_{d}(q)
.
In particular,
G_{\alpha}
is
3
-transitive on
\Delta
.
Then, noting that
\mathrm{A}_{n}=\mathrm{A}_{n-2}G_{\alpha}
, we have a factorization

T=\mathrm{A}_{n-2}T_{\alpha}
with
T_{\alpha}=\mathrm{SL}_{d}(q){:}\langle\sigma^{e}\rangle
.
Moreover,
T_{\alpha}
is a
2
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
.
Recalling that
n\geq 8
, we have
n-2\geq 6
.
Then the argument in Case 1
works for the triple
(n-1,n-2,r)
. Thus, noting that
n=q^{d}
, the only possibility is that
n=8
,
r=7
, and
T_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
.
Then
G_{\alpha}=2^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
, which has odd index in
\mathrm{A}_{8}
, a contradiction.
∎

5.
Graphs with solvable vertex-stabilizers

In this section, we deal with the graphs
described as in Hypothesis
4.1
under the further assumption that
G_{\alpha}
is solvable. We always let
n=|G:T|
, and view
G
as a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
(acting on
\Delta=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
).

By
[
27
, Theorem 1.1]
, combining Table
2.3
and Lemma
2.10
,
G
,
T
and
G_{\alpha}
are listed as in Table
5.7
up to isomorphism.

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{|l|l|c|c|c|l|}\hline\cr\mbox{Line}&G&T&G_{\alpha}&r&\\
\hline\cr 1&\mathrm{L}_{2}(r)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathbb{Z}_{r}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{r-1\over 2}&r\in\{11,19,29,59\}&\\
\hline\cr 2&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathbb{Z}_{11}&11&\\
\hline\cr 3&\mathrm{L}_{2}(19)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathbb{Z}_{19}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{3}&19&\\
\hline\cr 4&\mathrm{L}_{2}(29)&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathbb{Z}_{29}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{7}&29&\\
\hline\cr 5&\mathrm{A}_{c}&\mathrm{A}_{c-1}&&&c\geq 6\\
\hline\cr 6&\mathrm{A}_{6}&\mathrm{A}_{5}&&r=3&\\
\hline\cr 7&\mathrm{M}_{c}&\mathrm{M}_{c-1}&&&c\in\{12,24\}\\
\hline\cr 8&\mathrm{M}_{23}&\mathrm{M}_{22}&\mathbb{Z}_{23},\mathbb{Z}_{23}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{11}&23&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 5.7.

Note that, since
G_{\alpha}
is solvable, if
r\geq 5
then
G_{\alpha}

has a unique Sylow
r
-subgroup, which is semiregular on
\Delta
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume that
Hypothesis
4.1
holds and
G_{\alpha}
is solvable.
Let
n=|G:T|
.
Then
n
is divisible by
r
, and one of the following holds.

(1)

\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
for
r\in\{11,19,29,59\}
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(r)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{r}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{r-1\over 2}
.

(2)

n=11
,
r=11
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{11}
.

(3)

n=203
,
r=29
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(29)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{29}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{7}
.

(4)

n=r=23
,
G=\mathrm{M}_{23}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{22}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{23}
.

(5)

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
,
n\geq 7
,
r\geq 5
and
r^{2}
is not a divisor of
n
.

(6)

G=\mathrm{A}_{48}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{47}
,
r=3
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathrm{S}_{4}
.

Proof.

First, Lines 1, 2 and 4 of Table
5.7
give parts (1), (2) and (3) of this lemma, respectively. For Line 3 of Table
5.7
,
G
has a unique conjugacy class of elements of order
3
. This yields that
T\cap G_{\alpha}
contains
an element of order
3
, see
[
17
, Lemma 2.2]
for example. Then

3420=|G|={|T||G_{\alpha}|\over|T\cap G_{\alpha}|}\leq 1140
, a contradiction. For Line 8 of Table
5.7
, if

G_{\alpha}
has order
253
, then
G_{\alpha\beta}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{11}
for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, and
|\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})|
has order
55
(confirmed by GAP), which contradicts Lemma
2.9
. Thus
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{23}
, and part (4) of this lemma follows.

Suppose that Line 6 of Table
5.7
occurs. Noting that
G_{\alpha}
is known as in Table
2.3
, we have

G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{3}
or
\mathrm{S}_{4}
, and

|V|=60
or
15
respectively.
Since
\Gamma
has odd valency,
|V|
is even, and so
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{3}
. Then
|G|=|T||G_{\alpha}|
, and so

T\cap G_{\alpha}=1
.
Thus
G_{\alpha}
acts regularly on
\Delta
. This yields that

G_{\alpha}
contains odd permutations of
\mathrm{S}_{6}
, a contradiction.

Suppose that Line 7 of Table
5.7
occurs. Then

n=c\in\{12,24\}
.
If
r\geqslant 5
then the Sylow
r
-subgroup of
G_{\alpha}
is semiregular on
\Delta
, and so
r
is a divisor of
n
,
which is impossible. Thus we have
r=3
.
If
T\cap G_{\alpha}=1
then
\Gamma
is isomorphic to a symmetric
Cayley graph of
T
, and so
T
is normal in
G
by
[
29
]
, which is impossible. Therefore,
T\cap G_{\alpha}\neq 1
.
Recalling that
n=|G:T|=|G_{\alpha}:(T\cap G_{\alpha})|
, it follows that
n
is a proper divisor of
|G_{\alpha}|
. By Table
2.3
,

(n,G_{\alpha})
is one of

(12,\mathrm{S}_{4})
,
(12,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
(24,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. With the help of GAP, up to conjugation,
\mathrm{A}_{n}
has at most three transitive subgroups isomorphic to
G_{\alpha}
, and none of them
has a Sylow
2
-subgroup
P
such that
|\langle G_{\alpha},x\rangle|=|\mathrm{M}_{n}|
for some
x\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{n}}(P)
. Noting that
P=G_{\alpha\beta}
for some
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
,
we get a contradiction, see Lemma
2.9
.

Finally, suppose that Line 5 of Table
5.7
occurs. Then
n=c
, and
n=|G_{\alpha}:(T\cap G_{\alpha})|
as

G_{\alpha}
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.
If
r\geq 5
then the Sylow
r
-subgroup of
G_{\alpha}
is semiregular on
\Delta
, and so
n
is divisible by
r
, yielding part (5) of this lemma.
Assume that
r=3
. Then

G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{3},\,\mathrm{D}_{12}
,
\mathrm{S}_{4}
or
\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Suppose that
T\cap G_{\alpha}\neq 1
. Recalling that
6\leq n=|G_{\alpha}:(T\cap G_{\alpha})|
, we have
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{D}_{12},\,\mathrm{S}_{4}
or
\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. It follows that
(n,G_{\alpha})
is one of
(6,\mathrm{D}_{12})
,
(6,\mathrm{S}_{4})
,

(8,\mathrm{S}_{4})
,
(8,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
,
(16,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
,
(12,\mathrm{S}_{4})
,
(12,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
and
(24,\mathrm{S}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2})
. With the help of GAP, a similar argument as in the above paragraph leads to a contradiction. Thus
T\cap G_{\alpha}=1
. Then
\Gamma
is a Cayley graph of
\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
, and so part (6) of this lemma holds by
[
29
]
.
∎

Remark 5.1
.

For
r\in\{5,7\}
, there is a graph satisfying Theorem
5.1
(5) if and only if
(n,r,|G_{\alpha}|)
is one of
(20,5,40)
,
(40,5,40)
,
(40,5,80)
,
(80,5,80)
,
(7,7,7)
,
(7,7,21)
,
(21,7,21)
,
(21,7,63)
,
(63,7,63)
and
(84,7,84)
. This is confirmed by GAP.

Corollary 5.1
.

Assume that
Hypothesis
4.1
holds, and that
G
is isomorphic to a linear simple group of prime dimension. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
for
r\in\{11,19,29,59\}
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(r)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{r}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{r-1\over 2}
.

(2)

n=11
,
r=11
,
G=\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{11}
.

(3)

n=203
,
r=29
,
G\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(29)
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{29}{:}\mathbb{Z}_{7}
.

Proof.

Since
G=TG_{\alpha}
and
T
is nonabelian simple, by
[
27
, Corollary 3.4]
,
G_{\alpha}
is solvable. By Theorem
5.1
,
either one of parts (1)-(3) follows, or

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
,
r\geq 5
and
r
is a divisor of
n
. For the latter, we have
G\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(9)
but
n=6
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

Assume that
Hypothesis
4.1
holds.
Then
G
is not (isomorphic to) a simple unitary group of odd dimension.

Proof.

Suppose that
G\cong\mathrm{U}_{2m+1}(q)
.
Then, by Theorem
5.1
,
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable.
Thus
G=AB
for maximal subgroups
A
and
B
of
G
such that
one of
A
and
B
contains
T
and the other one contains
G_{\alpha}
. Then, checking the pairs
(A,B)
given in

[
31
, pp. 13, Table 3]
, the only possibility is that
G\cong\mathrm{U}_{9}(2)
,
A\cong\mathrm{J}_{3}
and
B\cong[2^{15}]{:}\mathrm{U}_{7}(2)
. If
T\leq B
then
G=AT
;
however,

|A|_{2}|T|_{2}\leq 2^{28}<2^{36}=|G|_{2}
, a contradiction. Thus
T\leq A
and
G_{\alpha}\leq B
.
It is easy to check that
|B|
is indivisible by
19
or
17
, and
|A|
is indivisible by
43
or
11
. Check the maximal subgroups of
A
and
\mathrm{U}_{7}(2)
, refer to the Atlas
[
5
]
. We conclude that
T=A
and
\mathrm{U}_{7}(2)\preccurlyeq G_{\alpha}
,
which contradicts Lemma
4.2
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Graphs arising from alternating groups

In this section, using some known factorizations of simple groups, we investigate the graphs in Hypothesis
4.1
under the further assumption that
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable and either
G
or
T
is isomorphic to an alternating group. Let
n=|G:T|
, view
G
as a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}

acting on
\Delta=[G:T]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Assume that
Hypothesis
4.1
holds.
Let
n=|G:T|
.
Assume that
|\Gamma(\alpha)|=r\geq 5
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
. Then
one of the following holds.

(1)

G=\mathrm{A}_{6}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{L}_{2}(4)
and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{6}
.

(2)

n>{r\choose 2}
,
and either
n\geq{r\choose{r-1\over 2}}
or
r\geq 11
and
n
is divisible by
r\choose l
for some
l
with
2\leq l<{r-1\over 2}
. Moreover, one the following holds:

(i)

\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
for
r\geq 7
,
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
, and
T
is isomorphic to a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
;

(ii)

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
and
G_{\alpha}
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.

Proof.

Note that
n>r
,
2n
is a divisor of
r!
and
\mathrm{A}_{r}
has a subgroup
H
of index
n
. Let
k={r-1\over 2}
.
Suppose that
n<{r\choose k}
. By
[
7
, Theorem 5.2A]
, either

\mathrm{A}_{r-l}\lesssim H\lesssim(\mathrm{S}_{l}\times\mathrm{S}_{r-l})\cap\mathrm{A}_{r}
for
some
l
with
2\leq l<k
, or
(r,k,n,H)
is one of
(5,2,6,\mathrm{D}_{10})
and
(7,2,15,\mathrm{L}_{3}(2))
.
For
(r,k,n,H)=(5,2,6,\mathrm{D}_{10})
, we have
G=\mathrm{A}_{6}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{L}_{2}(4)
and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{6}
as in part (1) of this lemma. Assume that
(r,k,n,H)=(7,2,15,\mathrm{L}_{3}(2))
.
Up to equivalence,
\mathrm{A}_{7}
has two permutation representations of degree
15
. Given such a representation of
G_{\alpha}
, using GAP, we search the
2
-elements
x
in
\mathrm{A}_{15}\setminus G_{\alpha}
which normalize a subgroup
\mathrm{A}_{6}
of
G_{\alpha}
. Computation shows that

\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle\cong\mathrm{A}_{8}
for each a desired
x
. We have
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{8}
; however, a subgroup of index
15
in
\mathrm{A}_{8}
is isomorphic to
2^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
, which can not play the role of
T
, a contradiction. Thus we assume next that either
n\geq{r\choose{r-1\over 2}}
, or
n<{r\choose{r-1\over 2}}
and
n
is divisible by
r\choose l
for
some
l
with
2\leq l<{r-1\over 2}
.
Moreover, for the latter case,
r\geq 11
. In fact, if
r\in\{5,7\}
and
n<{r\choose{r-1\over 2}}
then
(n,r)=(21,7)
, which is excluded by calculation in a similar way as above.

Suppose that
n={r\choose 2}
. Then either
r=5
or
r\geq 11
.
Let
M
be a maximal subgroup of
G_{\alpha}
with
H\leq M
.
Assume that
M
is a primitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r}
. Then
|H|\leq|M|<4^{r}
by

[
39
]
, and so
{r\choose 2}=n>{r!\over 4^{r}2}
, yielding
r\leq 13
. Then
(n,r)=(10,5)
,
(21,7)
,
(55,11)
and
(78,13)
. However, for such an
r
, every primitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r}
has index indivisible by
{r\choose 2}
, a contradiction.
Thus
M
is an intransitive or imprimitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r}
.
It follows from
[
30
]
that

M
is the stabilizer of some
2
-subset of
\{1,2,\ldots,r\}
, and thus
H=M\cong\mathrm{S}_{r-2}
.
Then the action of
G_{\alpha}
on
\Delta=[G:T]
is equivalent that
on the
2
-subsets of an
r
-set.
Thus, for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, the arc-stabilizer
G_{\alpha\beta}
has
two orbits on
\Delta
, say
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, which have length
r-1
and
(r-1)(r-2)\over 2
respectively.
Moreover, it is easily shown that
G_{\alpha\beta}
acts primitively on
both
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
. Noting that
|\Delta_{1}|\neq|\Delta_{2}|
, we know that

\mathbf{C}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
fixes both
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
set-wise. By
[
7
, Theorem 4.2A]
,
\mathbf{C}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
acts
trivially on both
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, yielding

\mathbf{C}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})=1
. Recalling
r\neq 7
, we have
\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})\lesssim\mathrm{S}_{r-1}
. Note that the transposition
(1\,2)
induces a product of
r-1
transpositions on
\Delta
. This implies that

\mathrm{S}_{r}\lesssim\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha})
, and thus
\mathbf{N}_{G_{\alpha}}(G_{\alpha\beta})\gtrsim\mathrm{S}_{r}
. Then
\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})\leq G_{\alpha}
, a contradiction. Thus
n>{r\choose 2}
.

Since
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
and
G=TG_{\alpha}
, by
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
,
one of the following occurs:

(i)

G\cong\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
,
T
is isomorphic to a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
;

(ii)

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
and
G_{\alpha}
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
;

(iii)

G\cong\mathrm{A}_{r+k}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
, and
T
is isomorphic to a
k
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r+k}
, where
2\leq k\leq 5
;

(vi)

the triple

(G,r,T)
is one of
(\mathrm{M}_{12},5,\mathrm{M}_{11})
,
(\Omega_{8}^{+}(2),7,\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2))
,
(\mathrm{A}_{15},13,\mathrm{A}_{13})
,
(\mathrm{A}_{15},13,\mathrm{A}_{8})
and
(\mathrm{A}_{15},7,\mathrm{A}_{13})
.

For the case (i) above,
\Gamma
is the complete graph
\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
.
If
r=5
then
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{6}
, yielding
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
, and so

6=n>{5\choose 2}=10
, which is impossible. Then
r\geq 7
, desired as in part (i) of this lemma.
For the case (ii) above, we have part (ii) of this lemma.
To complete the proof, we next exclude the left two cases.

Case 1
. Assume that case (iii) occurs. Consider the action of
G
on an
(r+k)
-set,
and take a
G_{\alpha}
-orbit with maximal length, say
m
. Then

m\geq r
, and
G_{\alpha}
has a subgroup
K
of index
m
.
Clearly,
m\leq r+k\leq 2r\leq{r\choose 2}
.
Suppose that
m={r\choose 2}
. Then
r(r-1)=2m
, the only possibility is that
r=5
and
m=10
. This implies that

k=r=5
. Thus
T
is (isomorphic to) a
5
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{10}
. This yields that
T=\mathrm{A}_{10}=G
, a contradiction.
Therefore,
|G_{\alpha}:K|=m<{r\choose 2}
and
m\leq r+5
.

By
[
7
, Theorem 5.2A]
,
either
(r,m)=(5,6)
, or
K\lesssim\mathrm{A}_{r-1}
. For the latter case,

r
is a divisor of
m=|G_{\alpha}:K|
, since
m\leq r+k
and
2\leq k\leq 5
, we have
r=k=5
, which yields that
T
is a
5
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{10}
, a contradiction. If
r+k\geq 9
and
r=5
then
k\geq 4
, and we have a similar contradiction as above.
All in all,
(r,m)=(5,6)
and
7\leq r+k<9
.
Computation using GAP shows that, for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, there is no
2
-element
x\in\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
G=\langle G_{\alpha},x\rangle
, which contradicts Lemma
2.9
.

Case 2
. Assume that case (iv) occurs.
Suppose that
(G,r,T)=(\Omega_{8}^{+}(2),7,\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2))
. Then, as a permutation group on
\Delta
, the group
G
has
2
transitive subgroups
\mathrm{A}_{7}
up to conjugacy, confirmed by GAP. Taking
G_{\alpha}
as any one of them, further calculation shows that there is no
2
-element
x\in\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
x^{2}\in G_{\alpha\beta}
and
G=\langle G_{\alpha},x\rangle
,
which contradicts Lemma
2.9
. Suppose that
(G,r,T)=(\mathrm{M}_{12},5,\mathrm{M}_{11})
. Then

G_{\alpha}
is a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{12}
acting on a
12
-set. Note that, up to equivalence,
G_{\alpha}
has a
unique permutation representation of degree
12
, and

G_{\alpha\beta}
is regular under this representation, where
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
. Computation shows that there is no
x\in\mathbf{N}_{\mathrm{A}_{12}}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
x^{2}\in G_{\alpha\beta}
and
|\mathrm{M}_{12}|=|\langle G_{\alpha},x\rangle|
,
a contradiction.

For the left three triples, we conclude that
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
has a
2
-transitive permutation representation of degree
15
. It follows that
r=7
. Considering the transitive permutation representations of
\mathrm{A}_{7}
of degree
15
, we get a similar contradiction by calculation.
This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\Gamma
,
G
,
T
and
r
be as in Hypothesis
4.1
, and let
n=|G:T|
.
Assume that
G
or
T
is isomorphic to an alternating group, and that
G_{\alpha}
is insolvable, where
\alpha\in V
. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

G=\mathrm{A}_{6}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
G_{\alpha}=\mathrm{L}_{2}(4)
,
r=5
and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{6}
.

(2)

G\cong\mathrm{M}_{12}
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{11}
,
r=11
and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{12}
.

(3)

G
,
T
and
G_{\alpha}
are given in Table
4.6
.

(4)

G=\mathrm{A}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{A}_{n-1}
,
G_{\alpha}
is a transitive but not
2
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
.

(5)

n>{r\choose 2}
for
r\geq 7
,
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{r+1}
,
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
,
T
is isomorphic to a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{r+1}
, and either
n\geq{r\choose{r-1\over 2}}
or
r\geq 11
and
n
is divisible by
r\choose l
for some
l
with
2\leq l<{r-1\over 2}
.

Proof.

If
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
then, by Lemma
6.1
one of parts (1), (4) and (5) occurs. Thus we assume next that
G_{\alpha}\not\cong\mathrm{A}_{r}
, and
discuss in two cases:
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{c}
for some
c\geq 5
;
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{c+1}
for some
c\geq 5
, but
T
is not isomorphic to an alternating group.

Case 1
. Assume that
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{c}
for some
c\geq 5
. By
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
, combining Lemma
4.2
, one line of the following table holds.

\tiny\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr G&T&G_{\alpha}&r\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{M}_{12}&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathrm{M}_{11}&11\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{A}_{15}&\mathrm{A}_{7},\mathrm{A}_{8}&\mathrm{S}_{13}&13\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{L}_{4}(3)&\mathrm{A}_{6}&3^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)&13\\
\hline\cr\Omega_{7}(3)&\mathrm{A}_{8},\mathrm{A}_{9}&[3^{6}]{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)&13\\
\hline\cr\Omega_{7}(3)&\mathrm{A}_{9}&3^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)&13\\
\hline\cr\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)&\mathrm{A}_{9}&[2^{4}]{:}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(4)\leq G_{\alpha}\leq[2^{6}]{:}\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{2}(4)\mbox{ (see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{567}{}{}]})}&5\\
\hline\cr\mathrm{A}_{c+k}&\mathrm{A}_{c}&k\mbox{-transitive subgroup of }\mathrm{A}_{c+k},1\leq k\leq 5&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

If
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{12}
then
\Gamma
is the complete graph
\mathsf{K}_{12}
, and part (2) of this lemma follows.

For

G\cong\mathrm{A}_{15}
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{13}
, the action of
G
on
V
is equivalent to that on the
2
-subsets of a
15
-set, and thus
G_{\alpha}
has no orbit of length
13
on
V
, a contradiction.
For

G\cong\mathrm{L}_{4}(3)
and
G_{\alpha}\cong 3^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)
, the action of
G
on
V
is
2
-transitive, yielding
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{40}
, which is not the case.

Suppose that
G\cong\Omega_{7}(3)
. If
G_{\alpha}\cong[3^{6}]{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)
then
G
is a primitive group on
V
with subdegrees
1
,
39
,
351
and
729
, and so
G_{\alpha}
has no orbit of length
13
on
V
, a contradiction. Let
G_{\alpha}\cong 3^{3}{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)
. Then
G_{\alpha\beta}\cong[3^{5}]{:}2\mathrm{S}_{4}
for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
. Checking the maximal subgroups of
G
in the Atlas
[
5
]
,
G_{\alpha}
is contained in a maximal subgroup
[3^{6}]{:}\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)
or
\mathrm{L}_{4}(3).2
. Computation shows that
|\langle x,G_{\alpha}\rangle|\leq 2|\mathrm{L}_{4}(3)|<|G|
for

x\in\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
x^{2}\in G_{\alpha\beta}
, a contradiction.
For
G\cong\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
, we know that
G_{\alpha}
is one of
[4^{2}]{:}\mathrm{SL}_{2}(4)
,
[4^{2}]{:}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(4)
,
[4^{2}]{:}\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{2}(4)
,
[4^{3}]{:}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(4)
and
[4^{3}]{:}\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{2}(4)
, see
[
26
, Theorem 3.4]
for example.
Confirmed by GAP, all subgroups of
\Omega^{+}_{8}(2)
isomorphic to
G_{\alpha}

are conjugate under
\mathbf{Aut}(G)
. Then calculation gives a similar contradiction as above.

Now
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{c+k}
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{c}
and
G_{\alpha}
is (isomorphic to) a
k
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{c+k}
, where
1\leq k\leq 5
. If
k=1
then
n=c+1
, and thus either part (3) of this lemma occurs by Lemma
4.3
when
G_{\alpha}
is a
2
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{n}
or, otherwise we have part (4) of this lemma.
Now let
k>1
. Then Lemma
4.3
works for the triple
(\mathrm{A}_{c+k},\mathrm{A}_{c+k-1},G_{\alpha})
. In this case, the only possibility is that
c+k=12
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
and so
\Gamma
is desired as in part (3) of this lemma.

Case 2
.
Assume that
G\cong\mathrm{A}_{c+1}
and
T
is not (isomorphic to) an alternating group. By
[
31
, Theorem D]
, combining Lemma
4.2
,
T
is a
k
-homogeneous subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{c+1}
, and either
r>5
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{r}
or

r=5
and
c+1<9
,
where
1\leq k\leq 5
and
c+1=r+k
. If
k=1
then
c=r
, and so
\mathrm{S}_{5}\lesssim\mathrm{A}_{6}
, which is impossible. Then
k\geq 2
.

Consider the action of
G
on a
(c+1)
-set, and take a
G_{\alpha}
-orbit with maximal length, say
m
. Then

m\geq r
, and
G_{\alpha}
has a subgroup
K
of index
m
.
Clearly,
m\leq c+1\leq 2r\leq{r\choose 2}
.
Suppose that
m={r\choose 2}
. Then
r(r-1)=2m
, the only possibility is that
r=5
and
m=10
. This implies that

k=r=5
, and
c+1=10
. Thus
T
is (isomorphic to) a
5
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{10}
, yielding
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{10}\cong G
, a contradiction.
Therefore,
|G_{\alpha}:K|=m<{r\choose 2}
and
m\leq r+5
.

By
[
7
, Theorem 5.2B]
,

(r,m)=(5,6)
or
K\lesssim\mathrm{S}_{r-1}
. For the latter case,

r
is a divisor of
m=|G_{\alpha}:K|
, since
m\leq c+1
and
2\leq k\leq 5
, we have
r=k=5
, which yields that
T
is a
5
-transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{A}_{10}
, a contradiction. Thus we have
(r,m)=(5,6)
and
7\leq r+k<9
, yielding
c+1=8
.
Computation shows that, for
\beta\in\Gamma(\alpha)
, there is no
x\in\mathbf{N}_{G}(G_{\alpha\beta})
with
x^{2}\in G_{\alpha\beta}
and
G=\langle G_{\alpha},x\rangle
, which contradicts Lemma
2.9
.
∎

In the following theorem, we consider the case where
G
is a sporadic simple group.

Theorem 6.2
.

Assume that
Hypothesis
4.1
holds, and
let
n=|G:T|
.
Assume that

G
is isomorphic to a sporadic simple group. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

n=r=23
,
G=\mathrm{M}_{23}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{22}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{23}
.

(2)

n=12
,
r=11
,
G=\mathrm{M}_{12}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{11}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{11}
, and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{12}
.

(3)

r=11
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{12}
,
T\cong\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{11}
, and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{12}
.

(4)

r=11
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{12}
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{11}
, and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{12}
.

(5)

r=23
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{24}
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{23}
, and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{24}
.

(6)

r=23
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{24}
,
T\cong\mathrm{L}_{2}(23)
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{23}
, and
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{24}
.

(7)

n=24
,
r=7
,
G=\mathrm{M}_{24}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{23}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
, and
\Gamma
is unique up to isomorphism.

Proof.

By Lemmas
4.3
and
6.1
and Theorems
5.1
and
6.1
, one of parts (1)-(3) holds if one of the following cases occurs: (i)
G_{\alpha}
is primitive on
\Delta
; (ii)
G_{\alpha}
is solvable; (iii) one of
T
and
G_{\alpha}
is an alternating group. Thus we suppose that none of (i)-(iii) occurs.
By
[
17
, Theorem 1.1]
, combining Lemma
4.2
, the following cases are left:

(iv)

r=c-1
,
G\cong\mathrm{M}_{c}
,
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{M}_{c-1}
,
c\in\{12,24\}
, and
T
is a transitive subgroup of
M_{c}
acting on
c
points.

(v)

G=\mathrm{M}_{n}
,
T=\mathrm{M}_{n-1}
,
n\in\{12,24\}
, and
G_{\alpha}
is a transitive subgroup of
M_{n}
.

Assume that (iv) occurs. Then
\Gamma\cong\mathsf{K}_{c}
and, in this case,
n>c
as
G\leq\mathrm{A}_{n}
. By
[
16
]
,
(c,T)
is one of
(12,\mathrm{L}_{2}(11))
,
(24,\mathrm{L}_{2}(7))
and
(24,\mathrm{L}_{2}(23))
, which gives one of parts (4)-(6) of this lemma, respectively.

Assume that (v) occurs. Combining Lemma
4.2
, calculation using GAP shows that either
n=12
and
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{S}_{5}
, or
n=24
and
G_{\alpha}
is isomorphic to one of the following groups:
\mathrm{S}_{5}
(twice up to the conjugacy in
\mathrm{M}_{24}
),

2^{2}\times\mathrm{A}_{5}
(twice),
(2^{2}\times\mathrm{A}_{5}){:}2
(twice),

\mathrm{A}_{4}\times\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
2^{4}\times\mathrm{A}_{5}
,
(\mathrm{A}_{4}\times\mathrm{A}_{5}){:}2
,

2^{6}{:}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(4)
,
2^{6}{:}\mathrm{\Gamma L}_{2}(4)
,
\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
,
2^{6}{:}(3\times\mathrm{L}_{3}(2))
and
2^{6}{:}(3\times\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)){:}2
.
Consider the normalizer
N
of a Hall
r^{\prime}
-subgroup of
G_{\alpha}
in
\mathrm{M}_{n}
. Since
N
contains a
2
-element which together with
G_{\alpha}
generate
\mathrm{M}_{n}
, it follows from a GAP calculation
that all cases but
G_{\alpha}\cong\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
are excluded.
The group
\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
has two subgroups of index
7
up to conjugation, which are isomorphic to
\mathrm{S}_{4}
.
Embedding
\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
in
\mathrm{M}_{24}
as a transitive subgroup, one
\mathrm{S}_{4}
of
\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)
is self-normalized in
\mathrm{M}_{24}
, and the second one has normalizer
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathrm{S}_{4}
. Then we have a unique

\Gamma
arising from the second
\mathrm{S}_{4}
, and thus part (7) of this lemma follows.
∎

By the argument in Section
5
and this section, our task is reduced to those graphs which satisfy
the following assumptions.

Hypothesis 6.1
.

\Gamma=(V,E)
,
G
,
T
and
r
satisfy Hypothesis
4.1
, and

(IV)

G
is a classical simple group over some finite field,

G_{\alpha}
has a unique insolvable composition factor, say
S=\mathbf{soc}(G_{\alpha}^{\Gamma(\alpha)})
, where
\alpha\in V
;

(V)

G\not\cong\mathrm{A}_{5},\,\mathrm{A}_{6},\,\mathrm{A}_{8}
,
\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
(with
m
a prime) or
\mathrm{U}_{2m+1}(q)
, and neither
T
nor

G_{\alpha}
is isomorphic to an alternating group.

7.
Some technique lemmas

The following two lemmas are derived from

[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, which bring a lot of convenience for us to determine the composition factor
S
of
G_{\alpha}
in Hypothesis
6.1
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
S
be a nonabelian simple group with a subgroup of prime index
r
.
Let
q=p^{f}
for a prime
p
and integer
f\geq 1
, and
let
H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)
for some
m\geq 2
.
Suppose that
H
has a unique insolvable composition factor
S
.
If

\Phi^{*}_{mf}(p)\neq 1
and
|H|
is divisible by
\Phi^{*}_{mf}(p)
then one of the following holds.

(1)

S=\mathrm{L}_{d}(q^{m\over d})
,
|H|_{p}
is a divisor of
{m\over d}|S|_{p}
, and either
(m,d,q)=(2,2,11)
or
d
is a prime and
mf
is a power of
d
.

(2)

S
,
m
and
q
are listed in Table
7.8
.

\tiny\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr S&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathrm{A}_{7}&\mathrm{A}_{11}&\mathrm{A}_{13}&\mathrm{A}_{19}&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)\\
\hline\cr(m,q)&(4,2)&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)&(18,2)&(10,2)&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)\\
&(4,3)&(6,5)&&&&&(6,5)&(5,4)&\\
&&(4,2)&&&&&(3,9)&&\\
&&(4,7)&&&&&(3,25)&&\\
&&(3,25)&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 7.8.

Proof.

Assume that
\Phi^{*}_{mf}(p)\neq 1
and
|H|
has a divisor
\Phi^{*}_{mf}(p)
.
By
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, ignoring those
H
which does not produce an suitable
S
, one of the following holds:

(1.1)

H
has a normal subgroup
\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)
(with
m=2
) or
\Omega^{-}_{4}(q)
(with
m=4
);

(1.2)

H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{m\over b}{.}[b]
, where
b
is a proper divisor of
m
;

(1.3)

S
,
m
and
q
are listed in Table
7.8
.

We only need to deal with cases (1.1) and (1.2).

Assume that (1.1) holds. Then either
S=\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)
, or
r
is one of
{q^{m}-1\over q-1}
,
q+1
and
q^{2}+1
respectively.
By Lemma
2.1
, for the latter case,
m
is a prime and

mf
is a power of
m
, or
m=4
and
2f
is a power of
2
.
Moreover, it is easy to see that
|H|_{p}=|S|_{p}
.
Then part (1) of this lemma occurs.

Assume that case (1.2) occurs. Let
H_{1}=H\cap\mathrm{GL}_{m\over b}(q^{b})
. Then
S\preccurlyeq H_{1}
, and
|H|_{p}
is a divisor of
|H_{1}|_{p}b
. By
[
31
, pp. 38,Proposition B]
,
\Phi^{*}_{mf}(p)
is a divisor of
|H_{1}|
. Applying
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]

to
H_{1}
and
\Phi^{*}_{{m\over b}bf}(p)
, we have

(2.1)

H_{1}
has a normal subgroup
\mathrm{SL}_{m\over b}(q^{b})
,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q^{b})
(with
b={m\over 2}
) or
\Omega^{-}_{4}(q^{b})
(with
b={m\over 4}
); or

(2.2)

H_{1}\leq\mathrm{GL}_{m\over ab}{.}[a]
, where
a
is a proper divisor of
{m\over b}
; or

(2.3)

(S,{m\over b},q^{b})
is one of
(\mathrm{A}_{7},3,25)
,
(\mathrm{L}_{3}(2),3,9)
,
(\mathrm{L}_{3}(2),3,25)
,
(\mathrm{L}_{2}(11),5,4)
.

First, all triples in (2.3) are included in Table
7.8
.

Assume that (2.1) occurs. Then
q^{b}\neq 11
as
b>1
, and
|H_{1}|_{p}=|S|_{p}
. Then
|H|_{p}
is a divisor of
b|S|_{p}
.
By Lemma
2.1
,
S
is described as in part (1) of this lemma.

Assume that case (2.2) occurs. By
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
and an induction on
m
, we conclude that
S=\mathrm{L}_{d}(p^{m\over d})
,
\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{m\over 2})
or
\mathrm{P\Omega}^{-}_{4}(q^{m\over 4})
. Then, by Lemma
2.1
,
S
is described as in part (1) of this lemma.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
S
be a nonabelian simple group with a subgroup of prime index
r
.
Let
q=p^{f}
for a prime
p
and integer
f\geq 1
, and let

H\leq\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)
for some
m\geq 3
. Suppose that
H
has a unique insolvable composition factor
S
.
If

\Phi^{*}_{(m-1)f}(p)\neq 1
and
|H|
is divisible by
\Phi^{*}_{(m-1)f}(p)
then one of the following holds.

(1)

S=\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
,
m
is a prime and
f
is a power of
m
.

(2)

S=\mathrm{L}_{d}(q^{m-1\over d})
,
|H|_{p}
is a divisor of
{m-1\over d}q^{m-1}|S|_{p}
, and either
(m-1,q)=(2,11)
or
d
is a prime and
(m-1)f
is a power of
d
.

(3)

S
,
m
and
q
are listed in Table
7.9
.

\tiny\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr S&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathrm{A}_{7}&\mathrm{A}_{11}&\mathrm{A}_{13}&\mathrm{A}_{19}&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{M}_{23}&\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)\\
\hline\cr(m,q)&(5,2)&(7,3)&(11,2)&(13,2)&(19,2)&(5,3)&(11,2)&&&\\
&(5,3)&(7,5)&&(11,2)&&&&&&\\
&&(5,2)&&&&&&&&\\
&&(5,3)&&&&&&&&\\
&&(4,2)&&&&&&&&\\
&&(4,9)&&&&&&&&\\
&&(4,25)&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr(m-1,q)&(4,2)&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)&(18,2)&(10,2)&&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)\\
&(4,3)&(6,5)&&&&&&(6,5)&(5,4)&\\
&&(4,2)&&&&&&(3,9)&&\\
&&(4,7)&&&&&&(3,25)&&\\
&&(3,25)&&&&&&&&\\
&&(3,2)&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 7.9.

Proof.

Assume that
\Phi^{*}_{(m-1)f}(p)\neq 1
and
|H|
has a divisor
\Phi^{*}_{(m-1)f}(p)
.
By
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, one of the following holds:

(1.1)

H
has a normal subgroup
\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
;

(1.2)

H\leq G_{1}:=q^{m-1}{:}((q-1)\times\mathrm{GL}_{m-1}(q))
;

(1.3)

S
,
m
and
q
are listed in Table
7.9
.

Case (1.1) gives part (1) of this lemma occurs. If (1.3) occurs then
(S,m,q)
is given as in part (3) of this lemma.
For case (1.2), by Lemma
7.1
, either
S
is described as in part (2) of this lemma, or
(S,m-1,q)
is listed in Table
7.9
.
∎

Under Hypothesis
6.1
, let
A
and
B
be maximal subgroups of
G
such that one of them contains
T
and the other one contains
G_{\alpha}
.
Then we have a maximal factorization

G=AB
with two factors
A
and
B
insolvable.

Employing
[
31
, Theorem A]
,
we will work on the possible candidates for the triple
(G,T,G_{\alpha})
.
An essential part of our approach is determine the inclusion of
G_{\alpha}
in
A
or
B
.
Suppose that
G_{\alpha}\leq B
and
B
has an almost simple quotient
B/N=L.O
, where
L
is simple.
Assume further that
|G:A|
has a prime divisor which does not divide
|O||N|
. By Lemma
2.6
, either

S\preccurlyeq B
, or we have a core-free
maximal factorization of
B/N
with a factor admitting
S
as a composition factor. Then, by Lemma
2.7
and
[
31
, Theorem A]
, we may determine
S
in a recursive process.
We use some notation
defined in
[
31
, pp.5]
, such as
\mathsf{P}_{i},\,\mathsf{N}_{i},\,\mathsf{N}_{i}^{-},\,\mathsf{N}_{i}^{+}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
S
be a nonabelian simple group with a subgroup of prime index
r
.
Let
q=p^{f}
for a prime
p
and integer
f\geq 1
,
L=\mathrm{U}_{2m}(q)
and
\mathbf{soc}(G_{0})=L
for some
m\geq 2
.
Suppose that
G_{0}=A_{0}B_{0}
is a core-free maximal factorization,
A_{0}\cap L=\mathsf{N}_{1}
and
G_{0}=A_{0}Y
for
Y\leq B_{0}
.
Suppose that
S
is the unique insolvable composition factor of
Y
, and
\pi(S)=\pi(Y)
. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

S=\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{m})
,
p=2
,
r=q^{m}+1
and
mf
is a power of
2
.

(2)

S
,
2m
and
q
are listed in Table
7.10
.

\tiny\begin{array}[]{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}\hline\cr S&\mathrm{A}_{5}&\mathrm{A}_{7}&\mathrm{A}_{11}&\mathrm{A}_{13}&\mathrm{A}_{19}&\mathrm{M}_{11}&\mathrm{L}_{3}(2)&\mathrm{L}_{2}(11)&\mathrm{L}_{3}(3)\\
\hline\cr(2m,q)&&&&&&&(6,2)&&(6,2)\\
\hline\cr(2m,q)&(4,2)&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)&(18,2)&(10,2)&(6,3)&(10,2)&(12,2)\\
&(4,3)&(6,5)&&&&&(6,5)&&\\
&&(4,7)&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr(m,q^{2})&&(3,25)&&&&&(3,25)&(5,4)&\\
&&&&&&&(3,9)&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 7.10
.

Proof.

By
[
31
, Theorem A, Tables 1 and 3]
,
|G_{0}:A_{0}|=q^{2m-1}{q^{2m}-1\over q+1}
and
L\cap B_{0}
is described as follows:

\tiny\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|l|l|l|l}L\cap B_{0}&\mathsf{P}_{m}&\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q){.}a&{}^{\bf\hat{}}\mathrm{SL}_{m}(4).2&{}^{\bf\hat{}}\mathrm{SL}_{m}(16).[6]&\mathrm{U}_{4}(3){.}2,\mathrm{M}_{22}&\mathrm{Suz}\\
\hline\cr&&a\leq 2&q=2,m\geq 3&q=4,G_{0}\geq L{.}4&q=2,m=3&q=2,m=6\\
\end{array}

Noting that
Y^{\infty}\unlhd L\cap Y
, it follows that
S\preccurlyeq L\cap Y
.
Since
LY\leq G_{0}
, it follows that
|Y:(L\cap Y)|
is a divisor of
o:=|G_{0}:L|
.
By Lemma
2.4
,
|Y|
is divisible by
|G_{0}:A_{0}|
.
Then
|L\cap Y|
has a divisor
q^{2m-1}{q^{2m}-1\over o(q+1)}
.

Assume that
L\cap B_{0}\cong\mathrm{Suz}
. Then
|(L\cap B_{0})\cap Y|
is divisible by
2^{11}\cdot 5\cdot 7\cdot 13
. By the Atlas
[
5
]
, we have
(L\cap B_{0})\cap Y\lesssim\mathrm{G}_{2}(4)
. Again by the Atlas
[
5
]
,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(4)
has no maximal subgroup of order divisible
